# Right-wing populism

"National Populism" redirects here. For the 2018 book by Roger Eatwell and Matthew Goodwin, see [National Populism: The Revolt Against Liberal Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Populism:_The_Revolt_Against_Liberal_Democracy).









From left to right, top to bottom:&#x20;

- Former Prime Minister of Hungary [Viktor Orbán](750582.md) and President of Argentina [Javier Milei](56599840.md)

- President of Turkey [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan)

- Prime Minister of Italy [Giorgia Meloni](29228976.md)

- President of the United States [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and Prime Minister of Israel [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md)

**Right-wing populism**, also called **right populism** and **national populism**,[^cite_note-Berman-1][^cite_note-2][^cite_note-\:2-3] is a [political ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology#Political_ideologies) that combines [right-wing politics](56522.md) with [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) rhetoric and themes. Its rhetoric employs [anti-elitist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-elitism) sentiments, opposition to [the Establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Establishment), and speaking to or for the [common people](24710172.md). Recurring themes of this political ideology include [neo-nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-nationalism), [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism), [economic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_nationalism), and [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism).[^cite_note-4] Frequently, they aim to defend a national culture, [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity), and [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy) against perceived attacks by outsiders.[^cite_note-5]

Some right-wing populist parties and movements have associations with elements of [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism),[^cite_note-JODweyland2-6][^cite_note-7] while some far-right populists draw comparisons to [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism).[^cite_note-\:6-8] Right-wing populism in the [Western world](21208200.md) is sometimes associated with ideologies such as [anti-environmentalism](8149492.md),[^cite_note-9] [anti-globalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-globalization_movement),[^cite_note-Rydgren_2018-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] [nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)),[^cite_note-Rydgren_2018-10][^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14] and [protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism).[^cite_note-15] In Europe, the term is often used to describe groups, politicians and political parties generally known for their [opposition to immigration](6491626.md),[^cite_note-Rydgren_2018-10][^cite_note-16] and for [Euroscepticism](10037.md).[^cite_note-17] Some right-wing populists may support expanding the [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state), but only for those they deem fit to receive it;[^cite_note-18] this concept has been referred to as "[welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism)".[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] Since the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession),[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] European right-wing populist movements began to grow in popularity,[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28] in large part due to increasing opposition to immigration from the Middle East and Africa, rising Euroscepticism and discontent with the economic policies of the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-29] Some forms of right-wing populism may also go into [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism) themes, with [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism), anti-[central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_bank), pro-[sound money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_currency) and the non-aggression principle being key themes in the libertarian spectre of right-wing populism, with [Ron Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Paul) and some parts of the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement) being a notable example of this.

From the 1990s, right-wing populist parties became established in the legislatures of various democracies. Right-wing populism has remained the dominant political force in the [Republican Party](32070.md) in the United States since the 2010s.[^cite_note-30] Although [extreme right-wing](221220.md) movements in the United States (where they are normally referred to as the "[radical right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(United_States\))") are usually characterised as separate entities, some writers consider them to be a part of a broader, right-wing populist phenomenon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaplanWeinberg19981–2-31] Examples of politicians described as right-wing populists include [Donald Trump](4848272.md)[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-trumppopulist-32][^cite_note-Global_Politics-33] and [Marine Le Pen](572897.md).[^cite_note-short-35]

## Definition

See also: [Populism § Authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism#Authoritarianism)

Right-wing populism is an ideology that primarily espouses [neo-nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-nationalism), [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) and [economic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_nationalism).[^cite_note-36]

The political scientist [Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde) argues that what he calls the "populist radical right" starts with the idea of 'the nation'. He however rejects the use of [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) as a core ideology of right-wing populism on the ground that there are also purely "civic" or "liberal" forms of nationalism, preferring instead the term *[nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\))*: a xenophobic form of nationalism asserting that "states should be inhabited exclusively by members of the native group ('the nation'), and that non-native elements (persons and ideas) are fundamentally threatening to the homogeneous nation-state". Mudde further argues that "while nativism could include racist arguments, it can also be non-racist (including and excluding on the basis of culture or even religion)", and that the term *nativism* does not reduce the parties to mere single-issue parties, such as the term *anti-immigrant* does. In the maximum definition, to nativism is added authoritarianism—an attitude, not necessarily anti-democratic or autocratic, to prefer "[law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\))" and the submission to authority[^cite_note-37]—and [populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism)—a "thin-centered ideology" that considers society to be ultimately separated into two homogeneous and antagonistic groups, "the pure people" versus "the corrupt elite", and which argues that politics should be an expression of the "general will of the people", regardless of human rights or constitutional guarantees.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Cas Mudde and Cristóbal Rovira Kaltwasser reiterated in 2017 that within European right-wing populism, there is a "marriage of convenience" of populism based on an "ethnic and chauvinistic definition of the people", authoritarianism, and nativism. This results in right-wing populism having a "xenophobic nature".[^cite_note-short-35]

[Roger Eatwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Eatwell), emeritus Professor of Comparative Politics at the [University of Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bath), writes that, "whilst populism and fascism differ notably ideologically, in practice the latter has borrowed aspects of populist discourse and style, and populism can degenerate into leader-oriented authoritarian and exclusionary politics."[^cite_note-\:6-8] According to left-wing media [Vice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_Media), for populism to transition into fascism or proto-fascism it requires a "[nihilistic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nihilism) and an intractable crisis".[^cite_note-\:7-40]

> \[P]opulism is like fascism in being a response to liberal and socialist explanations of the political. And also like fascism, populism does not recognize a legitimate political place for an opposition that it regards as acting against the desires of the people and that it also accuses of being tyrannical, conspiratorial, and antidemocratic. ... The opponents are turned into public enemies, but only rhetorically. If populism moves from rhetorical enmity to practices of enemy identification and persecution, we could be talking about its transformation into fascism or another form of dictatorial repression. This has happened in the past ... and without question it could happen in the future. This morphing of populism back into fascism is always a possibility, but it is very uncommon, and when it does happen, and populism becomes fully antidemocratic, it is no longer populism.[^cite_note-41]

Erik Berggren and Andres Neergard wrote in 2015 that, "\[m]ost researchers agree \[...] that [xenophobia](34141.md), [anti-immigration sentiments](6491626.md), [nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)), [ethno-nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_nationalism) are, in different ways, central elements in the ideologies, politics, and practices of right-wing populism and Extreme Right Wing Parties."[^cite_note-42] Similarly, the historian Rick Shenkman describes the ideology presented by right-wing populism as "a deadly mix of xenophobia, racism, and authoritarianism".[^cite_note-43] Tamir Bar-On also concluded in 2018 that the literature generally places "nativism" or "ethnic nationalism" as the core concept of the ideology, which "implicitly posits a politically dominant group, while minorities are conceived as threats to the nation". It is "generally, but not necessarily racist";[^cite_note-44] in the case of the Dutch [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom) for instance, "a religious \[minority, i.e. Muslims] instead of an ethnic minority constitutes the main 'enemy'".[^cite_note-45]

Scholars use terminology inconsistently, sometimes referring to right-wing populism as "[radical right](221220.md)"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaplanWeinberg199810–11-46] or other terms such as [new nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-nationalism).[^cite_note-47] [Pippa Norris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pippa_Norris) noted that "standard reference works use alternate typologies and diverse labels categorising parties as '[far](221220.md)' or 'extreme' right, '[New Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Right)', '[anti-immigrant](6491626.md)' or '[neo-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism)', '[anti-establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-establishment)', 'national populist', 'protest', 'ethnic', 'authoritarian', '[anti-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-statism)', 'anti-party', '[ultranationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultranationalism)', '[right-libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarianism)' and so on".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorris200544-48] The term *authoritarian populism* can be used to describe right-wing populism,[^cite_note-d214-49] although it is also used to refer to left-wing political movements.[^cite_note-u165-50][^cite_note-y618-51]

In regard to the authoritarian aspect of right-wing populism, the [political psychologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_psychologist) Shawn W. Rosenberg asserts that its "intellectual roots and underlying logic" are best seen as "a contemporary expression of the fascist ideologies of the early 20th century".

> Guided by its roots in ideological fascism ... and its affinity to the fascist governments of 1930s Germany and Italy, \[right-wing populism] tends to delegate unusual power to its leadership, more specifically its key leader. This leader embodies the will of the people, renders it clear for everyone else and executes accordingly. Thus distinctions between the leadership, the people as a whole and individuals are blurred as their will is joined in a single purpose. (p.5) ... In this political cultural conception, individuals have a secondary and somewhat derivative status. They are rendered meaningful and valued insofar as they are part of the collective, the people and the nation. Individuals are thus constituted as a mass who share a single common significant categorical quality – they are nationals, members of the nation. ... In this conception, the individual and the nation are inextricably intertwined, the line between them blurred. As suggested by philosophers of fascism ... the state is realized in the people and the people are realized in the state. It is a symbiotic relation. Individuals are realized in their manifestation of the national characteristics and by their participation in the national mission. In so doing, individuals are at once defined and valued, recognized and glorified. (p. 12)[^cite_note-52]

According to Rosenberg, right-wing populism accepts the primacy of "the people", but rejects liberal democracy's protection of the rights of minorities, and favors ethno-nationalism over the legal concept of the nation as a polity, with the people as its members; in general, it rejects the rule of law. All of these attributes, as well as its favoring of strong political leadership, suggest right-wing populism's fascist leanings.[^cite_note-rosenberg-53] The historian [Federico Finchelstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federico_Finchelstein) defines populism as a form of authoritarian democracy while fascism is an ultraviolent dictatorship.[^cite_note-54]

According to [Roger Eatwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Eatwell) and [Matthew Goodwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Goodwin_\(author\)), "National populists prioritize the culture and interests of the nation, and promise to give voice to a people who feel that they have been neglected, even held in contempt, by distant and often corrupt elites." They are part of a "growing revolt against mainstream politics and liberal values. This challenge is in general not anti-democratic. Rather, national populists are opposed to certain *aspects* of liberal democracy as it has evolved in the West. \[...] \[Their] 'direct' conception of democracy differs from the 'liberal' one that has flourished across the West following the defeat of fascism and which has gradually become more elitist in character." Furthermore, national populists question what they call the "erosion of the nation-state", "hyper ethnic change" and the "capacity to rapidly absorb \[high] rates of immigration", the "highly unequal societies" of the West's current economic settlement. They are suspicious of "cosmopolitan and globalizing agendas".[^cite_note-\:2-3]

Populist parties use crises in their domestic governments to enhance anti-globalist reactions; these include refrainment towards trade and anti-immigration policies. The support for these ideologies commonly comes from people whose employment might have low occupational mobility. This makes them more likely to develop an anti-immigrant and anti-globalisation mentality that aligns with the ideals of the populist party.[^cite_note-55]

[Jean-Yves Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Camus) and [Nicolas Lebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Lebourg) see "national populism" as an attempt to combine the socio-economical values of the left and political values of the right and the support for a [referendary](51582.md) republic that would bypass traditional political divisions and institutions as they aim for the unity of the political (the *demos*), ethnic (the *ethnos*) and social (the *working class*) interpretations of the "people", national populists claim to defend the "average citizen" and "common sense", against the "betrayal of inevitably corrupt elites".[^cite_note-\:0-56] As the [Front National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Front_National) ideologue [François Duprat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Duprat) put in the 1970s, inspired by the Latin American right of that time, right-populism aims to constitute a "national, social, and popular" ideology. If both left and right parties share [populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) itself, their premises are indeed different in that right-wing populists perceive society as in a state of decadence, from which "only the healthy common people can free the nation by forming one national class from the different social classes and casting aside the corrupt elites".[^cite_note-57]

Methodologically, by co-opting concepts from the left—such as [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism) and [ethnopluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnopluralism), which is espoused by the left as a means of preserving minority ethnic cultures within a pluralistic society—and then jettisoning their non-hierarchical essence, right-wing populists can, in the words of the sociologist [Jens Rydgren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Rydgren), "mobilize on xenophobic and racist public opinions without being stigmatized as racists".[^cite_note-58] The sociologist [Hande Eslen-Ziya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hande_Eslen-Ziya) argues that right-wing populist movements rely on "troll science", namely "(distorted) scientific arguments moulded into populist discourse" that creates an alternative narrative.[^cite_note-59] In addition to rhetorical methods, right-wing populist movements have also flourished by using tools of digital media, including websites and newsletters, social media groups and pages, as well as [YouTube](3524766.md) and messaging chat groups.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61][^cite_note-\:3-62]

### Cultural issues and immigration

While immigration is a common theme at the center of many national right-wing populist movements, the theme often crystallizes around cultural issues, such as religion, gender roles, and sexuality, as is the case with the transnational anti-gender theory movements.[^cite_note-\:3-62][^cite_note-63] A body of scholarship has also found populist movements to employ or be based around conspiracy theories, rumors, and falsehoods.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] Some scholars argue that right-wing populism's association with conspiracy, rumor and falsehood may be more common in the digital era thanks to widely accessible means of content production and diffusion.[^cite_note-67] These media and communication developments in the context of specific historical shifts in immigration and cultural politics have led to the association of right-wing populism with [post-truth politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-truth_politics).[^cite_note-\:3-62]

## History

### Germany and France (1870–1900)

German and French right-wing populism can be traced back to the period 1870–1900 in the aftermath of the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War), with the nascence of two different trends in Germany and France: the [*Völkisch* movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkisch_movement) and [Boulangism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boulangism).[^cite_note-\:1-68] *Völkischen* represented a [romantic nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_nationalism), [racialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racialism) and, from the 1900s, [antisemitic](1078.md) tendency in German society, as they idealised a bio-mystical "original nation" that still could be found in their views in the rural regions, a form of "primitive democracy freely subjected to their natural elites".[^cite_note-\:1322-69][^cite_note-\:1-68] In France the anti-parliamentarian [Ligue des Patriotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ligue_des_Patriotes), led by [Georges Ernest Boulanger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georges_Ernest_Boulanger), [Paul Déroulède](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_D%C3%A9roul%C3%A8de) and [Maurice Barrès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Barr%C3%A8s), called for a "plebiscitary republic", with the president elected by [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage), and the popular will expressed not through elected representatives (the "corrupted elites"), but rather via "legislative plebiscites", another name for referendums.[^cite_note-\:1-68] It also evolved to antisemitism after the [Dreyfus affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreyfus_affair) (1894).[^cite_note-70]

### Denmark and Norway (1970s)

Modern national populism—what Pierro Ignazi called "post-industrial parties"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnazi200226-71]—emerged in the 1970s, in a dynamic sustained by voters' rejection of the [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) and of the tax system, both deemed "confiscatory"; the rise of xenophobia against the backdrop of immigration which, because originating from outside Europe, was considered to be of a new kind; and finally, the end of the prosperity that had reigned since the post–Second World War era, symbolised by the [oil crisis of 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis). Two precursor parties consequently appeared in the early 1970s: the [Progress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Denmark\)), the ancestor of the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party), and [Anders Lange's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Norway\)) in Norway.[^cite_note-\:0-56]

### Netherlands and France (2001)

A new wave of right-wing populism arose after the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md). "Neo-populists" are nationalist and Islamophobic politicians who aspire "to be the champions of freedoms for minorities (gays, Jews, women) against the Arab-Muslim masses"; a trend first embodied by the Dutch [Pim Fortuyn List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pim_Fortuyn_List) and later followed by [Geert Wilders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geert_Wilders)' [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom) and [Jean Marie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen) and his daughter [Marine Le Pen](572897.md)'s [National Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Rally_\(France\)). According to [Jean-Yves Camus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Yves_Camus) and [Nicolas Lebourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Lebourg), those parties are not a real [syncretism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncretism) of the left and right, as their ideology and voter base are interclassist.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] Furthermore, neo-populist parties went from a critique of the welfare state to that of [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism), and their priority demand remains the reduction of immigration.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]

### Hungary (early 2000s)

In the early 2000s the [Jobbik Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jobbik), formally known as the Movement for a Better Hungary, emerged and rapidly became the country's most successful far-right political party. Jobbik, which was founded in 2003, exploited antisemitic and [anti-Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Roma_racism_in_Italy) feelings to rally support, as well as strong nationalist rhetoric and hostility to capitalism and liberalism. The party's successful use of internet channels to attract and mobilise young people resulted in tremendous popularity and influence.[^cite_note-\:8-76]

[Viktor Orbán](750582.md)'s [Fidesz Party](404647.md) is also a prominent factor in Hungarian right-wing populism. Under Orbán's leadership, Fidesz shifted from a centre-right party to a right-wing populist organisation centred on national sovereignty, anti-immigration policies, and conservative social values, frequently battling with the EU on a variety of topics. [Orbán's administration](81566732.md) had centralised authority, controlled media and altered legal frameworks to remain in power.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-\:8-76]

## By country

[Piero Ignazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piero_Ignazi?action=edit\&redlink=1), an Italian [political scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science), divided right-wing populist parties, which he called "extreme right parties", into two categories: he placed traditional right-wing parties that had developed out of the historical right and post-industrial parties that had developed independently. He placed the [British National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_Party), the [National Democratic Party of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Party_of_Germany), the [German People's Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_People's_Union), and the former Dutch [Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Netherlands\)) in the first category, whose prototype would be the disbanded [Italian Social Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Movement). In contrast, he placed the French [National Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Front_\(France\)), the German [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_\(Germany\)), the Dutch [Centre Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Democrats_\(Netherlands\)), the former Belgian [Vlaams Blok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Blok) (which would include certain aspects of traditional extreme-right parties), the Danish [Progress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Denmark\)), the Norwegian [Progress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Norway\)) and the [Freedom Party of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Party_of_Austria) in the second category.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnazi200226-71][^cite_note-google-78]

Right-wing populist parties in the English-speaking world include the [UK Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Independence_Party), [Reform UK](59757202.md), and the Australian [One Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Nation_\(Australia\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorris200568–69,_72-79] The US [Republican Party](32070.md), the [Conservative Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada), the British [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), the [Liberal Party of Australia](18453.md), and the [New Zealand National Party](333149.md) include right-wing populist factions.

#### Nigeria

[Rabiu Kwankwaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabiu_Kwankwaso) and his [New Nigeria People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Nigeria_People's_Party) are generally [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism)[^cite_note-80] and [ultraconservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism).[^cite_note-alj1-81] Styling himself off of [Aminu Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aminu_Kano), Kwankwaso has voiced support for the [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) and building more universities, while also increasing the size of the [Nigerian Armed Forces](21391.md) and [Nigerian Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Police_Force).[^cite_note-82] Kwankwaso is seen as being strongly [culturally conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_conservatism) and a deeply pious [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Nigeria), although he is no [Islamist](15012.md).[^cite_note-alj1-81] Even with Kwankwaso's cultural conservatism, he has expressed support for [women's rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_rights) [in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality_in_Nigeria#Feminism_in_Nigeria).[^cite_note-83]

#### South Africa

Main article: [Afrikaner nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaner_nationalism)

|  | This section **possibly contains original [synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:SYNTH).** Source material should [verifiably mention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Verifiability) and [relate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Content_removal#Irrelevant_information) to the main topic. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Right-wing_populism#). *(May 2024)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |

According to [John Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Campbell_\(diplomat\)) from the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations), [Freedom Front Plus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Front_Plus) is a [white](4734206.md) and [coloured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coloureds) dominated political party that promotes [Afrikaner nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaner_nationalism). The current party manifesto, written by [Pieter Groenewald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieter_Groenewald), calls for an end to [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action) and [Black Economic Empowerment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Economic_Empowerment) while supporting [proportional representation](23298.md).[^cite_note-\:5-84] Freedom Front Plus has always promoted policies which are conservative in nature and support [Afrikaans-speakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afrikaans) and [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_South_Africa) from the [Dutch Reformed Church of South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Reformed_Church_of_South_Africa).[^cite_note-\:5-84]

#### Uganda



Yoweri Kaguta Museveni, the President of Uganda

President [Yoweri Museveni](293409.md) and his party, the [National Resistance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Resistance_Movement), are usually considered right-wing populist,[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-lac1-86] [anti-LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_opposition),[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88] and [Ugandan](31816.md) [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism). According to Corina Lacatus, "Museveni came to power in 1986 as a populist figure who adopted an authoritarian leadership style and converted over the years in an authoritarian leader. Over the years, he has continued to rely on a tried-and-tested populist discourse that granted him political success in the first place, to continue the advancement of his regime and to promote his election campaigns."[^cite_note-lac1-86]

### Americas

#### Argentina



[President of Argentina](229473.md) Javier Milei

[Javier Milei](56599840.md), the incumbent president of Argentina, is known for his flamboyant personality, distinctive personal style, and strong media presence. Milei's views distinguish him in the [Argentine political landscape](67621.md) and have garnered him significant public attention and polarising reactions. He has been described politically as a [right-wing libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarianism) and right-wing populist who supports *[laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire)* economics, aligning specifically with [minarchist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night-watchman_state) and [anarcho-capitalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarcho-capitalism) principles. Milei has proposed a comprehensive overhaul of the country's fiscal and structural policies. He supports [freedom of choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_choice) on drug policy, firearms, prostitution, same-sex marriage, sexual preference and gender identity, while [opposing abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movements) and euthanasia. In foreign policy he advocates closer relations [with the United States](15961583.md), supporting Ukraine in response to the [Russian invasion of the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine). He is also distancing Argentina from geopolitical ties [with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentina%E2%80%93China_relations).[^cite_note-89]

Milei has been variously described as far-right,[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] far-right populist,[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94][^cite_note-95] right-wing libertarian,[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] [ultraconservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism),[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] and [ultra-liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_fundamentalism).[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] A philosophical anarcho-capitalist who is for practical purposes a [minarchist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night-watchman_state), Milei advocates minimal government, focusing on justice and security,[^cite_note-Il_Post_2023-105] with a philosophy rooted in life, liberty, and property, and [free-market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market) principles. He criticises [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism) and [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism),[^cite_note-Cronista_2019-106] advocating [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization) and the restructuring of government ministries.[^cite_note-107] He opposes the [Central Bank of Argentina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Argentina) and current taxation policies.[^cite_note-Centenera_&_Criales_2023-108][^cite_note-109]

Economically, Milei is influenced by the [Austrian school of economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_school_of_economics) and admires the former president [Carlos Menem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Menem)'s policies.[^cite_note-Fiore_Viani_2021-110] He supports [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism), viewing socialism as embodying envy and coercion.[^cite_note-Cronista_2019-106] Milei proposes reducing government ministries and addressing economic challenges through spending cuts and fiscal reforms, criticising previous administrations for excessive spending.[^cite_note-Viriglio_2023-111][^cite_note-112] He has praised the economic policies of the former British prime minister [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher) and called her "a great leader".[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

#### Brazil

See also: [Bolsonarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsonarism)



[Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md), Brazilian president from 2019 to 2023

In Brazil right-wing populism began to rise roughly around the time [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) won the [2014 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brazilian_general_election).[^cite_note-116] In [the 2014 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brazilian_general_election), [Levy Fidelix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levy_Fidelix), from the [Brazilian Labour Renewal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Labour_Renewal_Party),[^cite_note-117] presented himself with a conservative speech and, according to him, the only right-wing candidate. He spoke for traditional [family values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values) and opposed abortion, legalisation of cannabis, and same-sex marriage and proposed that homosexual individuals should be treated far away from the good citizens' and workers' families.[^cite_note-118] In the first round of the general election, Fidelix received 446,878 votes, representing 0.43% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-119] Fidelix ranked 7th out of 11 candidates. In the second round Fidelix supported [Aécio Neves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%A9cio_Neves).[^cite_note-120]

In addition, according to the political analyst of the Inter-Union Department of Parliamentary Advice, Antônio Augusto de Queiroz, the [National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Brazil) [elected in 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brazilian_general_election) may be considered the most conservative since the "re-democratisation" movement, noting an increase in the number of parliamentarians linked to more conservative segments, such as [ruralists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism), the [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Brazil), the [police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_of_Brazil) and the [religious right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_right). The subsequent economic crisis of 2015 and investigations of corruption scandals led to a right-wing movement that sought to rescue [fiscally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism) and [socially conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) ideas in [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_opposition) to the [left-wing policies](18499.md) of the [Workers' Party](224622.md). At the same time, [right-libertarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarianism), such as those that make up the [Free Brazil Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Brazil_Movement), emerged among many others. For Manheim (1952), within a single real generation, there may be several generations which he called "differentiated and antagonistic". For him, it is not the common birth date that marks a generation, although it matters, but rather the historical moment in which they live in common. In this case, the historical moment was the [impeachment of Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Dilma_Rousseff). They can be called the "post-Dilma generation".[^cite_note-auto-121]

The centrist interim president [Michel Temer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Temer) took office following the impeachment of President [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff). Temer held 3% approval ratings in October 2017,[^cite_note-phillips2-122] facing a corruption scandal after accusations of obstructing justice and [racketeering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racketeering) against him.[^cite_note-watson2-123] He managed to avoid trial thanks to the support of the right-wing parties in the [National Congress of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Brazil).[^cite_note-phillips2-122][^cite_note-watson2-123] On the other hand, President of the Senate [Renan Calheiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renan_Calheiros), acknowledged as one of the key figures behind Rousseff's destitution and a member of the centrist [Brazilian Democratic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Democratic_Movement), was removed from office after facing [embezzlement](209411.md) charges.[^cite_note-124]

In March 2016, after entering the [Social Christian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Christian_Party_\(Brazil\)), the far-right congressman [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) decided to run for President of the Republic. In 2017 he tried to become the presidential nominee of [Patriota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriota), but, eventually, Bolsonaro entered the [Social Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Liberal_Party_\(Brazil\)) and, supported by the [Brazilian Labour Renewal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Labour_Renewal_Party), he won the [2018 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_general_election), followed by the left-wing former Mayor of São Paulo [Fernando Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Haddad) of [Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](99328.md)'s Workers' Party.[^cite_note-CN10918-125][^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] Lula was banned from running after being convicted on criminal corruption charges and imprisoned.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] Bolsonaro has been accused of racist,[^cite_note-Gavin_Fernando-130] xenophobic,[^cite_note-131] misogynistic[^cite_note-132] and homophobic rhetoric. His campaign was centred on opposition to crime, political corruption and queer identity, and support for tax cuts, [militarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarism), [Catholicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicity) and [evangelicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism).[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134]

#### Canada



[Doug Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Ford)

In recent years, right-wing populist elements have existed within the Conservative Party of Canada and mainstream provincial parties and have been espoused by Ontario MP [Kellie Leitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kellie_Leitch); the businessman [Kevin O'Leary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_O'Leary); [Quebec Premier](370751.md) [François Legault](6899647.md); the former Mayor of Toronto [Rob Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rob_Ford); and his brother, Ontario Premier [Doug Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Ford).[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136][^cite_note-dford-137][^cite_note-economist-138][^cite_note-139] Doug Ford endorsed [Donald Trump](4848272.md) during the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) and publicly expressed support for him during [his first presidency](52231341.md).[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] He again supported Trump in the 2024 election, but later opposed him after that election and expressed regret for supporting him.[^cite_note-142]

In August 2018 the Conservative MP [Maxime Bernier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxime_Bernier) left the party, and the following month he founded the [People's Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_of_Canada), which has self-described as "smart populism" and been described as a "hard-right populist" party by Canadian journalists.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] Bernier lost his seat in the 2019 Canadian federal elections, and the People's Party scored just above 1% of the vote; however, in the 2021 election, it saw improved performance and climbed to nearly 5% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-complex-145]



[Pierre Poilievre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Poilievre)

[Pierre Poilievre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Poilievre), who has been described as populist by some journalists,[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-populism1-147] won the [2022 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election) and became the leader of both the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada) and the [Official Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_\(Canada\)). Some journalists have compared Poilievre to American [Republican](32070.md) populists such as [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz);[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-populism1-147] however, many journalists have dismissed these comparisons due to Poilievre's pro-choice, pro-immigration and pro-[same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Canada) positions.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156] Zack Beauchamp writing for *[Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\))* in 2024 described Poilievre as a "tame populist" and stated that he does not fit the far right because "on policy substance, he's actually considerably more moderate than Trump or European radicals" and "he's unwilling to attack immigrants and ethnic minorities in the way that others in the global far right do".[^cite_note-Beauchamp-157] Beauchamp further described "relatively neutered populism" as a strength of Canada's politics rather than a failure.[^cite_note-Beauchamp-157]

#### Chile



[José Antonio Kast](55052190.md)

[José Antonio Kast](55052190.md), [President of Chile](11885300.md) since 2026 and his Chilean right-wing [Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(Chile,_2019\)) have been labeled as right-wing populist.[^cite_note-158] [Johannes Kaiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kaiser_\(Chilean_politician\)), founder of the [National Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Libertarian_Party) and its candidate in the 2025 presidential election, has also been described as a right-wing populist.[^cite_note-159]

#### Costa Rica

In the [2018 political campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Costa_Rican_general_election), both the [Evangelical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism) Christian candidate [Fabricio Alvarado Muñoz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabricio_Alvarado_Mu%C3%B1oz)[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] and the right-wing anti-establishment candidate [Juan Diego Castro Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Diego_Castro_Fern%C3%A1ndez)[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] were described as examples of right-wing populists.

[Donald Trump](4848272.md)

In the United States right-wing populism is frequently aligned with evangelical Christianity,[^cite_note-164] [segregationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165] [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_nationalism), [nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\))[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165] [anti-intellectualism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-intellectualism)[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165] and [antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-RightWingPopAtlantic-166][^cite_note-167] The [Republican Party](32070.md), particularly supporters of Donald Trump, includes right-wing populist factions.[^cite_note-168]

Moore (1996) argues that "populist opposition to the growing power of political, economic, and cultural elites" helped shape "conservative and right-wing movements" since the 1920s.[^cite_note-169] Historical right-wing populist figures in both major parties in the United States have included [Thomas E. Watson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_E._Watson), [Strom Thurmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strom_Thurmond),[^cite_note-170] [Joseph McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_McCarthy), [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace) and [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan).[^cite_note-RightWingPopAtlantic-166]

Several of the prominent members of the [Populist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_\(United_States\)) of the 1890s and 1900s, while [economically progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_progressivism), supported social aspects of right-wing populism.[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165] Watson, the vice-presidential nominee of the Populist Party in [1896](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1896_United_States_presidential_election) and presidential nominee in [1900](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1900_United_States_presidential_election), eventually embraced [white supremacy](33178.md) and antisemitism.[^cite_note-NGE-171] William Jennings Bryan, the 1896 Populist presidential nominee, was socially and theologically conservative, supporting creationism, Prohibition and other aspects of [Christian fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_fundamentalism). Bradley J. Longfield posits Bryan was a "theologically conservative [Social Gospeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Gospel)".[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165][^cite_note-172] An article by [National Public Radio](29697232.md)'s Ron Elving likens the populism of Bryan to the later right-wing populism of Trump.[^cite_note-ElvingBryanTrump-165]

In 2010 Rasmussen and Schoen characterised the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement) as "a right-wing anti-systemic populist movement". They added, "Today our country \[the United States] is in the midst of a...new populist revolt that has emerged overwhelmingly from the right – manifesting itself as the Tea Party movement."[^cite_note-173] In 2010 [David Barstow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Barstow) wrote in *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, "The Tea Party movement has become a platform for conservative populist discontent."[^cite_note-174] Some political figures closely associated with the Tea Party, such as the US senator [Ted Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Cruz) and the former US representative [Ron Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Paul), have been described as appealing to right-wing populism.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] In the [US House of Representatives](19468510.md) the [Freedom Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Caucus), associated with the Tea Party movement, has been described as right-wing populist.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

[Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s presidential campaigns in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign), [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign), and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign), noted for their [anti-establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-establishment), anti-immigration and [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) rhetoric, have been characterised as right-wing populist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDolgert2016Greven2016-180][^cite_note-181] The ideology of Trump's former Chief Strategist [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon) has also been described as such.[^cite_note-182] Trump's policies and rhetoric have been frequently described as right-wing populist by academics and political commentators.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184]

### Asia-Pacific countries

#### Australia

See also: [Hansonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hansonism)



[Pauline Hanson](69926.md)

Right-wing populism in Australia often utilises narratives on having a national identity based on [settler colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism) with a deeply rooted thought on being the rightful occupants of the land and downplaying the presence and rights of [Indigenous Population](12598742.md) such as opposition to [Indigenous land rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_land_rights_in_Australia), and resistance to [Indigenous recognition](65267019.md).[^cite_note-185]

Right-wing populism has also been represented by [Pauline Hanson's One Nation](83013.md), led by [Pauline Hanson](69926.md), [Senator](165705.md) for [Queensland](59051.md)[^cite_note-186] and typically [preferences votes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party-preferred_vote) to the mainstream [Liberal-National Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_\(Australia\)),[^cite_note-187] and [Katter's Australian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katter's_Australian_Party), led by Queensland [MP](61565.md) [Bob Katter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Katter).[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

Furthermore, the main centre-right party the [Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_\(Australia\)) has certain members belonging to the right-wing populist faction known as [National Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Right_\(Liberal_Party_of_Australia\)) including the former opposition leader [Peter Dutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Dutton).[^cite_note-190]

#### China



[Xi Jinping](2017814.md), CCP General Secretary since 2012

In the 2010s [the wave of refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war) caused by the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md) caused a wave of anti-immigration sentiment on the [Chinese internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_China), and many narratives have been similar to those of the populist right have since been observed, such as anti-"[western leftism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baizuo)", [Islamophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobia_in_China) and [anti-multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_multiculturalism).[^cite_note-191] Since [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) became the [CCP General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) in 2012, the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) has been characterized by scholars as adapting views and policies closely related to right-wing populism, particularly on socio-cultural issues as well as [foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_Xi_Jinping).[^cite_note-jstor.org-192][^cite_note-Pretorius,_Christo_&_Valev,_Radoslav-193][^cite_note-Szatters,_Konrad-194][^cite_note-Mohammad,_Tazia-195]

In Japan, the [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md) and [Sanae Takaichi](8125532.md) administrations have been described as authoritarian populism or nationalistic populism.[^cite_note-196]

Right-wing populist parties in Japan encompasses two distinct factions. One faction includes conservatives such as [Nippon Ishin no Kai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Ishin_no_Kai), who are either unaffiliated with or opposed to the [Liberal Democratic Party](164231.md)'s (LDP) [1955 System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_System).[^cite_note-197] The other faction resembles Western far-right populists and includes parties such as [Japan First Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_First_Party), [Sanseitō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanseit%C5%8D), and the [Conservative Party of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Japan).[^cite_note-198]

#### New Zealand

Main article: [Populism in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism_in_New_Zealand)



[Winston Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Peters) of the [New Zealand First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_First) party

Right-wing populism is thought to have emerged in New Zealand with [Robert Muldoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Muldoon), the [New Zealand National Party](333149.md) prime minister from 1975 to 1984. An [economic nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_nationalism) and [social conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism), Muldoon has been cited as having appealed to the masses through his animosity towards the media and leftists and his own abrasive and colourful public persona.[^cite_note-\:4-199] He also often made rude or unusually frank comments about foreign leaders, including American president [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) and Australian prime minister [Malcolm Fraser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Fraser),[^cite_note-\:4-199] whom he ridiculed and even bullied.[^cite_note-200]

The [New Zealand First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_First) party, which has historically taken a [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist) standpoint, has been described as a populist party.[^cite_note-MuddeKaltwasser2012-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

#### Pakistan

In Pakistan, [Pakistan Tehreek Insaaf](1651925.md) (PTI) has recently been described as centrist-populist while sharing some characteristics with right-wing populists.[^cite_note-Daily_Times-204] Its leader [Imran Khan](413184.md) has attacked traditional politicians.[^cite_note-Daily_Times-204] The British journalist [Ben Judah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Judah), in an interview, compared Khan with [Donald Trump](4848272.md) on his populist rhetoric.[^cite_note-The_limits_of_populism-205]
