#### Taiwan

See also: [Han fans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_fans)

Taiwan's right-wing populists tend to deny the independent identity of their country's 'Taiwan' and emphasise their identity as a 'Republic of China'. Taiwan's [left-wing](16066160.md) [Taiwanese nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_nationalism) have strong pro-American tendencies, so Taiwan's major and minor conservatives are critical of this.[^cite_note-206] In particular, Taiwan's right-wing populists demand that economic growth and right-wing [Chinese nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism) issues should be more important than [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) and that they should become closer to the People's Republic of China. Some of Taiwan's leading right-wing populists include [Terry Gou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Gou), [Han Kuo-yu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Kuo-yu) and [Chang Ya-chung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang_Ya-chung).[^cite_note-KMT01-207][^cite_note-KMT02-208][^cite_note-209]

### European countries

Main article: [Radical right (Europe)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\))

In May 2019 *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* magazine described Ireland, Malta and Portugal as the only three European Union countries without far-right populist parties in their parliament.[^cite_note-n374-210] Portugal subsequently elected the [Chega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chega_\(political_party\)) party to its parliament in [October 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Portuguese_legislative_election).[^cite_note-z985-211] The French-speaking Belgian region of [Wallonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia) is also described as a rare place in Europe without a significant right-wing populism presence, in part due to a media [cordon sanitaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordon_sanitaire_\(politics\)) which prohibits far-right candidates from live media appearances.[^cite_note-n010-212][^cite_note-b581-213]

In 2016 senior EU diplomats cited growing anxiety in Europe about Russian financial support for far-right and populist movements and told the *[Financial Times](136566.md)* that the intelligence agencies of "several" countries had scrutinised possible links with Moscow.[^cite_note-214] Also in 2016, the [Czech Republic](5321.md) warned that Russia was trying to "[divide and conquer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divide_and_rule)" the EU by supporting right-wing populist politicians across the bloc.[^cite_note-215] A 2019 study shows a significant correlation between the price of housing and voting for populist parties in Europe.[^cite_note-216]

#### Belgium



[Tom Van Grieken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Van_Grieken), leader of [Vlaams Belang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Belang)

[Vlaams Blok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Blok), established in 1978, operated on a platform of law and order, anti-immigration (with a particular focus on Islamic immigration) and secession of the [Flanders](10878.md) region of the country. The secession was originally planned to end in the annexation of Flanders by the culturally and linguistically similar [Netherlands](21148.md) until the plan was abandoned due to the multiculturalism in that country. In the elections to the Flemish Parliament in June 2004, the party received 24.2% of the vote, within less than 2% of being the largest party.[^cite_note-belgium-217] However, in November of the same year, the party was ruled illegal under [the country's anti-racism law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Anti-Racism_Law) for, among other things, advocating segregated schools for citizens and immigrants.[^cite_note-218]

In less than a week, the party was re-established under the name [Vlaams Belang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Belang), initially with a near-identical ideology before moderating parts of its statute. It advocates the adoption of the Flemish culture and language by immigrants who wish to stay in the country. It also calls for a zero-tolerance stance on [illegal immigration](7124412.md) and the reinstatement of border controls.[^cite_note-vlaamsbelang-219] Despite some accusations of [antisemitism](1078.md) from Belgium's Jewish population, the party has demonstrated a staunch pro-Israel stance as part of its opposition to Islam.[^cite_note-haaretz-220] In [Antwerp](32149462.md), sections of the city's significant Jewish population have begun to support the party.[^cite_note-221] With 23 of 124 seats, Vlaams Belang leads the opposition in the Flemish Parliament.[^cite_note-vlaamsparlement-222] It also holds 11 out of the 150 seats in the [Belgian Chamber of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Representatives_\(Belgium\)).[^cite_note-223]

The Flemish nationalist and [conservative liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_liberalism) [New Flemish Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Flemish_Alliance) party has been described as populist or containing right-wing populist elements by foreign media such as the German *[Die Zeit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Die_Zeit)* magazine. However, the party has rebutted the term and does not label itself as such.[^cite_note-224]

In the French-speaking [Wallonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia), [Mischaël Modrikamen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mischa%C3%ABl_Modrikamen), an associate of [Steve Bannon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Bannon), was chairman of the [Parti Populaire (PP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_\(Belgium\)), which contested elections in Wallonia. Political analysts have generally observed that right-wing populist parties tend to perform better with the Flemish electorate over French-speaking Belgian voters, on the whole, owing to the Flemish vote moving to the right in recent decades and Flemish parties intertwining Flemish nationalism with other issues.[^cite_note-CN10918-125]

As of the 2019 federal, regional, and European elections, [Vlaams Belang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaams_Belang) has surged from 248,843 votes in 2014 to 783,977 on 26 May 2019.[^cite_note-225]

#### Denmark



[Pia Kjærsgaard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pia_Kj%C3%A6rsgaard), former leader of the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party) from 1995 to 2012. She held the post of [Speaker of the Folketing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_speakers_of_the_Folketing) from 2015 to 2019.

In the early 1970s the home of the strongest right-wing populist party in Europe was in Denmark, the [Progress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress_Party_\(Denmark\)).[^cite_note-Rydgren-226] In the [1973 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Danish_general_election) it received almost 16% of the vote.[^cite_note-teg-227] In the following years its support dwindled, but the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party) replaced it in the 1990s, becoming an important support party for the governing coalition in the 2000s.[^cite_note-228] At the height of its popularity it won 21% of the vote (corresponding to 37 seats) in the [2015 Danish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Danish_general_election),[^cite_note-229] becoming the second-largest party in the Folketing and serving once again as support party for two minority governments 2015–2019 before being reduced to 16 seats in the [2019 Danish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Danish_general_election) and 5 seats (2.6% of the vote) in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Danish_general_election).[^cite_note-DST-230] In 2015 the [New Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Right_\(Denmark\)) party was founded,[^cite_note-231] which gained six seats (3.7% of the vote) at the 2022 election.[^cite_note-DST-230] In 2022 the [Denmark Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denmark_Democrats) were founded as the most recent right-wing populist party in the Folketing, gaining 8% of the vote and 14 seats at the 2022 general election.[^cite_note-232]

#### Finland

In Finland the most popular right wing to far-right party is the [Finns Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party). The [most recent parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Finnish_parliamentary_election) took place on 2 April 2023. After the 2023 election the [Orpo Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orpo_cabinet) was formed by the [National Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Coalition_Party), [Finns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finns_Party) and [Swedish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_People's_Party_of_Finland) as well as the [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Finland\)).[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236]

#### France

See also: [Gaullism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism)



[Marine Le Pen](572897.md), leader of the [National Rally](67119.md) and [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election) and [2022 presidential candidate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_French_presidential_election)

[Gaullism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism) is considered part of (right-wing) populism because it is based on [charisma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charisma), popular mobilisation, [French nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationalism), and [exceptionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exceptionalism). Gaullism is deeply embedded in modern right-wing politics in France.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238]

The [National Front](67119.md) (NF)—renamed in 2018 as the "National Rally"—has been cited as the "prototypical populist radical right-wing party".[^cite_note-short-35] The party was founded in 1972 by [Jean-Marie Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Marie_Le_Pen) as the unification of several French nationalist movements of the time; he developed it into a well-organised party.[^cite_note-short-35] After struggling for a decade, the party reached its first peak in 1984. By 2002 Le Pen received more votes than the socialist candidate in the first round of voting for the French presidency, becoming the first NF candidate to qualify for a presidential [runoff election](25932.md). After Le Pen's daughter, [Marine Le Pen](572897.md), took over as the head of the party in 2011, the National Front established itself as one of the main political parties in France. Marine Le Pen's policy of "de-demonising" or normalising the party resulted in Jean-Marie being first suspended and then ejected from the party in 2015. Marine Le Pen finished second in the [2017 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election) and lost in the second round of voting versus [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md), which was held on 7 May 2017. However, polls published in 2018 showed that a majority of the French population considered the party to be a threat to democracy.[^cite_note-Nossiter-239]

Right-wing populism in France has also involved cultural issues such as the anti-gay marriage and anti-gender theory movements exemplified by [La Manif pour tous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Manif_pour_tous).[^cite_note-\:3-62]

#### Germany



[Alternative for Germany](38708229.md) is a political party, founded in 2013 and currently led by [Alice Weidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Weidel) and [Tino Chrupalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tino_Chrupalla), which has become Germany's largest right-wing populist party.

In 2005 a nationwide [Pro Germany Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_Germany_Citizens'_Movement) (*pro Deutschland*) was founded in [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne). The [Pro-movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-movement) appeared as a conglomerate of numerous small parties, voters' associations and societies, distinguishing themselves by campaigns against [extremism](428002.md)[^cite_note-240] and immigrants.

The AfD has grown in popularity, particularly in eastern Germany, where it has benefited from economic dissatisfaction and immigration fears. By the mid-2020s, opinion polls consistently placed the AfD as the second-strongest party nationally, with growing influence at both the federal and state level. The party's positions have become more radical over time, including calls for Germany to leave the EU and NATO and opposition to climate protection measures.[^cite_note-241]

In 2024 the AfD won the state election in [Thuringia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Thuringian_state_election) and finished a close second in [Saxony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Saxony_state_election) and [Brandenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Brandenburg_state_election).

The party's constitutional status has been a matter of ongoing legal dispute. In May 2025, Germany's domestic intelligence agency, the [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution) (BfV), classified the federal AfD as a "confirmed right-wing extremist" organization (*gesichert rechtsextremistisch*), the highest of three monitoring categories. The AfD challenged this classification in court, and in February 2026 the [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) Administrative Court provisionally barred the agency from applying or publicly stating this classification pending a full trial; as of mid-2026, the case remains unresolved, and the federal party is formally treated as a "suspected case" (*Verdachtsfall*) in the interim.[^cite_note-242] Separately, several state-level AfD branches — including in [Saxony](28395.md), [Thuringia](31130.md), [Saxony-Anhalt](38138.md), [Brandenburg](3765.md), and [Lower Saxony](18435.md) — have been classified as confirmed right-wing extremist by their respective state authorities, with some classifications judicially upheld.[^cite_note-243]

#### Greece



[Panos Kammenos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panos_Kammenos), leader of [Independent Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Greeks) and Greek [Minister for National Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_National_Defence_\(Greece\))

The most prominent right-wing populist party in Greece is the [Independent Greeks (ANEL)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Greeks).[^cite_note-Stefanova2014-244][^cite_note-KarnerMertens2013-245] Despite being smaller than the more extreme [Golden Dawn](1938004.md) party, after the [January 2015 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_Greek_legislative_election), ANEL formed a governing coalition with the left-wing [Coalition of the Radical Left (SYRIZA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriza), thus making the party a governing party and giving it a place in the [Cabinet of Alexis Tsipras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Cabinet_of_Alexis_Tsipras).[^cite_note-246]

The [Neo-Nazi](54361.md) Golden Dawn has grown significantly in Greece during the economic downturn, gaining 7% of the vote and 18 out of 300 seats in the [Hellenic Parliament](2449826.md). The party's ideology includes [annexing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irredentism) territory in Albania and Turkey, including the Turkish cities of [Istanbul](3391396.md) and [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir).[^cite_note-todayszaman-247] Controversial measures by the party included a poor people's kitchen in [Athens](1216.md), which only supplied Greek citizens and was shut down by the police.[^cite_note-248]

The [Popular Orthodox Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Orthodox_Rally) is not represented in the Greek legislature but supplied 2 of the country's 22 MEPS until 2014. It supports [anti-globalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-globalisation), lower taxes for small businesses, and opposition to [Turkish accession to the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Turkey_to_the_European_Union) and the [Republic of Macedonia](23564616.md)'s [use of the name Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_naming_dispute) and immigration only for Europeans.[^cite_note-249] Its participation in government has been one of the reasons why it became unpopular with its voters who turned to [Golden Dawn](1938004.md) in Greece's 2012 elections.[^cite_note-carleton-250]

#### Italy

See also: [Berlusconism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlusconism)



[Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi) (1936–2023)

In Italy, Prime Minister [Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi) served four separate terms between 1994 and 2011, and is considered the first prominent populist politician in modern Europe, fueling [anti-immigrant sentiments](6491626.md), denying the results of the [2006 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Italian_general_election),[^cite_note-251] and often making offensive comments towards the judiciary and political opponents,[^cite_note-252] although his [Forza Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forza_Italia_\(1994\)) party is considered to be more moderate.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254] Instead, the most prominent right-wing populist party in the last twenty years was the [Lega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lega_\(political_party\)), formerly [Lega Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lega_Nord) (LN),[^cite_note-spiegel-255] whose leaders reject the right-wing label,[^cite_note-ilfoglio-256][^cite_note-asca-257][^cite_note-termometropolitico-258] although not the "populist" one.[^cite_note-leganord-259]

The Lega is a [federalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism), [regionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(politics\)) and sometimes [secessionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secessionist) party, founded in 1991 as a federation of several regional parties of [northern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Italy) and [central Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Italy), most of which had arisen and expanded during the 1980s. LN's program advocates the transformation of Italy into a federal state, fiscal federalism, and greater regional autonomy, especially for the northern regions. At times, the party has advocated for the secession of the north, which it calls [Padania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padania). The party generally takes an anti-[southern Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Italy) stance as members are known for opposing southern Italian emigration to northern Italian cities, stereotyping southern Italians as welfare abusers and detrimental to Italian society, and attributing Italy's economic troubles and the disparity of the [north–south divide in the Italian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_divide_in_Italy) to supposed inherent negative characteristics of the southern Italians, such as laziness, lack of education, or criminality.[^cite_note-tambini-260][^cite_note-bullaro-261][^cite_note-willey-262][^cite_note-pullella-263] Certain LN members have been known to publicly deploy the offensive slur *[terrone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrone)*, a common pejorative term for southern Italians evocative of negative southern Italian stereotypes.[^cite_note-tambini-260][^cite_note-bullaro-261][^cite_note-garau-264]

With the rise of immigration into Italy since the late 1990s, LN has increasingly turned its attention to criticising mass immigration to Italy. The LN, which opposes [illegal immigration](7124412.md), is critical of [Islam](6037917.md), and proposes Italy's exit from the [Eurozone](184391.md), is considered a [Eurosceptic](10037.md) movement and, as such, is a part of the [Identity and Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_and_Democracy) (ID) group in the [European Parliament](9581.md). LN was or is part of the national government in 1994, 2001–2006, 2008–2011, and 2018–2019. Most recently, the party, including among its members the presidents of [Lombardy](43807.md) and [Veneto](43780.md), won 17.4% of the vote in the [2018 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Italian_general_election), becoming the third-largest party in Italy (largest within the [centre-right coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_coalition_\(Italy\))). In the [2014 European election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_European_Parliament_election_in_Italy), under the leadership of [Matteo Salvini](1767165.md), it took 6.2% of votes. Under Salvini, the party has, to some extent, embraced [Italian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_nationalism) and emphasised Euroscepticism, opposition to immigration, and other "populist" policies while allying with right-wing populist parties in Europe.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267]



Giorgia Meloni, prime minister since 2022

Between the late 2010s and the early 2020s, another right-wing populist movement emerged within the [centre-right coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_coalition_\(Italy\)). The nationalist and national-conservative [Brothers of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Italy) (FdI), led by [Giorgia Meloni](29228976.md), gained 4.4% of votes in the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Italian_general_election) and, four years later, it became the most voted party in the [2022 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Italian_general_election), gaining 26% of votes. Meloni was appointed prime minister on 22 October, at the head of what it was considered as the most right-wing Italian government since 1945.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269]

#### Netherlands



[Geert Wilders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geert_Wilders), leader of the [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom)

In the [Netherlands](21148.md), right-wing populism was represented in the 150-seat [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Netherlands\)) in 1982 when the [Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Party_\(Netherlands\)) won a single seat. During the 1990s a splinter party, the [Centre Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Democrats_\(Netherlands\)), was slightly more successful, although its significance was still marginal. Not before 2002 did a right-wing populist party break through in the Netherlands, when the [Pim Fortuyn List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pim_Fortuyn_List) (LPF) won 26 seats and subsequently formed a coalition with the [Christian Democratic Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Appeal) (CDA) and [People's Party for Freedom and Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_for_Freedom_and_Democracy) (VVD). [Pim Fortuyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pim_Fortuyn), who had strong views against immigration, particularly by Muslims, was assassinated in May 2002, two weeks before the election. Ideologically, the LPF differed somewhat from other European right-wing populist movements by holding more liberal stances on certain social issues such as abortion, gay rights, and euthanasia (Fortuyn himself was openly gay) while maintaining an uncompromising stance on immigration, law and order, and the EU. Fortuyn was also credited with shifting the Dutch political landscape by bringing the topics of multiculturalism, immigration, and the integration of immigrants into the political mainstream.[^cite_note-270]

Since 2006 the [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom) (PVV) has been represented in the House of Representatives and described as inheriting the mantle of the Pim Fortuyn List. Following the 2010 general election, it has been in a pact with the [right-wing minority government of CDA and VVD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Dutch_cabinet_formation#Formateur_Opstelten) after it won 24 seats in the House of Representatives. The party is Eurosceptic and plays a leading role in the changing stance of the Dutch government towards [European integration](683704.md) as they came second in the [2009 European Parliament election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_European_Parliament_election_in_the_Netherlands), winning 4 out of 25 seats. The party's main program revolves around strong [criticism of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom#Islam), restrictions on migration from new European Union countries and Islamic countries, pushing for cultural assimilation of migrants into Dutch society, opposing the [accession of Turkey to the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accession_of_Turkey_to_the_European_Union), advocating for the Netherlands to withdraw from the European Union and advocating for a return to the [guilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilder) and abandoning the euro.[^cite_note-271]

From 2017 onwards, the [Forum for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forum_for_Democracy) (FvD) emerged as another right-wing populist force in the Netherlands. The FvD also advocates a stricter immigration policy and a referendum on Dutch membership of the EU.[^cite_note-Heinrich_Böll_Foundation-272][^cite_note-273]

The [Farmer–Citizen Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farmer%E2%80%93Citizen_Movement), described as a right-wing populist party,[^cite_note-274] won the [2023 Dutch provincial elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Dutch_provincial_elections), winning the popular vote and receiving the most seats in all twelve provinces.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]

#### Poland



Poland's [Law and Justice](520309.md) (PiS) leader [Jarosław Kaczyński](637440.md) with Hungarian Prime Minister [Viktor Orbán](750582.md)

[^cite_note-277]

The largest right-wing populist party in Poland is [Law and Justice](520309.md). It combines [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) and [criticism of immigration](6491626.md) with strong support for [NATO](21133.md) and an [interventionist economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_intervention) policy.[^cite_note-278]

The other right-wing populist parties in Poland are [Confederation Liberty and Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation_Liberty_and_Independence) and the [Confederation of the Polish Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation_of_the_Polish_Crown).

#### Portugal



[André Ventura](60708639.md), founder and president of [Chega](60708659.md)

The political party [Chega](60708659.md) ('Enough!') has risen to prominence in Portugal throughout the early 2020s. Led by [André Ventura](60708639.md), the party has expressed anti-immigrant and Eurosceptic views.[^cite_note-279] Chega surged in support during the [2024 Portuguese legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Portuguese_legislative_election). In the [2025 Portuguese legislative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Portuguese_legislative_election) election, Chega won more seats than the [Socialist Party](403237.md) and became the main opposition, the first time since [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Portuguese_legislative_election) one of the two largest parties in Portugal were not the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_\(Portugal\)) or Socialist Party.

Ventura has consistently regarded the rise of Chega as an end to the two-party system in Portugal.[^cite_note-280]

#### Romania

The most prominent Romanian right-wing populist party, the [Alliance for the Union of Romanians](64666536.md) (AUR, in [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language): *Alianța pentru Unirea Românilor*), was founded in 2019 by [George Simion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Simion), an independent candidate for the [2019 Romanian European Parliament Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_European_Parliament_election_in_Romania). Running on a conservative nationalist platform, which was also described as [pro-Russian](2541825.md), and aiming for the [unification of Moldova with Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Moldova_and_Romania), the party became the fourth-largest political force in the country after the [2020 Romanian parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Romanian_parliamentary_election). Growing in popularity over the next four years, in the [2024 Romanian parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Romanian_parliamentary_election), the party became the second-largest political party in Romania and the leading party of the opposition.[^cite_note-ro-281][^cite_note-282]

The right-wing populist move of AUR prompted the founding of other nationalist parties, with diverse rates of success in the elections. The 2024 parliamentary elections oversaw the entering of the Parliament of Romania of two other right-wing to far-right parties, namely [S.O.S. Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S.O.S._Romania) (SOS, in Romanian: *S.O.S. România*) and the [Party of Young People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Young_People) (POT, in Romanian: *Partidul Oamenilor Tineri*). Both parties split from AUR and are led by former AUR members: [Diana Șoșoacă](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_%C8%98o%C8%99oac%C4%83) for SOS and [Anamaria Gavrilă](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anamaria_Gavril%C4%83) for POT. [S.O.S. Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S.O.S._Romania) has been often called to be "even worse" than AUR by activelly being Russophile, neo-Legionnaire and bearing a [Hard Eurosceptic position](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_Eurosceptic), calling for [the withdrawal of Romania from the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_withdrawal_from_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-283]

##### 2024 and 2025 Romanian presidential elections and aftermath

With Georgescu barred from candidating, both George Simion and Anamaria Gavrilă filled their candidacies on 14 March, announcing that, shall both candidacies be validated, one of them would withdraw in order to allow full support for one nationalist candidate. The one withdrawing was Anamaria Gavrilă, which publicly announced this on 19 March. In the elections taking place in May, George Simion won the first round by a wide margin, entering the second round with the [Save Romania Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_Romania_Union) (USR, in Romanian: *Uniunea Salvați România*) endorsed the independent mayor of Bucharest, [Nicușor Dan](35543359.md). Dan, who previously received less than two million votes, won by a narrow margin, being elected as [President of Romania](273659.md).[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285]

#### Spain



[Santiago Abascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Abascal), leader of [VOX](41928488.md) party since 2014

In Spain, the appearance of right-wing populism began to gain strength after the December 2018 [election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Andalusian_regional_election) for the [Parliament of Andalusia](12181806.md), in which the right-wing populist party [VOX](41928488.md) managed to obtain 12 seats[^cite_note-286] and agreed to support a coalition government of the parties of the right [People's Party](404727.md) and [Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_\(Spanish_political_party\)), although the [Socialist Party](272596.md) won the elections.[^cite_note-287] VOX, which has been frequently described as far-right, both by the left parties and by Spanish or international press,[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] promotes characteristic policies of the populist right,[^cite_note-290] such as the expulsion of all [illegal immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigrants) from the country—and of legal immigrants who commit crimes—a generalised criminal tightening, combined with traditional claims of right-wing conservatives, such as the centralisation of the state and the suppression of the [Autonomous Communities](55002.md), and has harshly criticised the laws against [gender violence](3634424.md), approved by the socialist government of [José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero](19614411.md), but later maintained by the PP executive of [Mariano Rajoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Rajoy), accusing the people and institutions that defend them of applying "gender totalitarianism".[^cite_note-291]

The party official [Javier Ortega Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_Ortega_Smith) is being investigated for alleged [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech) after Spanish prosecutors admitted a complaint by an Islamic association in connection with a rally that talked about "the Islamist invasion".[^cite_note-292]

After months of political uncertainty and protests against the party in [Andalusia](2736.md)[^cite_note-293] and other regions,[^cite_note-294] in the [2019 Spanish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2019_Spanish_general_election), VOX managed to obtain 24 deputies in the [Congress of Deputies](352254.md), with 10.26% of the vote, falling short of expectations[^cite_note-295] after an intense electoral campaign in which VOX gathered big crowds of people at their events. Although the People's Party and Citizens leaders, [Pablo Casado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Casado) and [Albert Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Rivera), had admitted repeatedly during the campaign that they would again agree with VOX to reach the government,[^cite_note-296] the sum of all their seats finally left them far from any possibility, giving the government to the social democrat [Pedro Sánchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_S%C3%A1nchez_\(politician\)).[^cite_note-297]

[Madrilenian](69802.md) president [Isabel Díaz Ayuso](59693424.md), despite being a member of the centre-right [People's Party](404727.md), has been sustained in government by VOX and adopted many policies championed by the party.[^cite_note-298] She has embraced populist rhetoric,[^cite_note-299] defended Spanish imperialism,[^cite_note-300] dismissed [climate change](5042951.md),[^cite_note-301] and opposed COVID-19 lockdowns.[^cite_note-302] She has been to compared to [Donald Trump](4848272.md) by several of her critics.[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

The [2024 European Parliament election in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_Parliament_election_in_Spain) saw the right-wing populist party [Se Acabó La Fiesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Se_Acab%C3%B3_La_Fiesta) (SALF) entering the European Parliament with 3 seats, obtaining over 800,000 votes.[^cite_note-305]

#### Sweden

In Sweden, the first openly populist movement to be represented in the [Riksdag](158651.md) (Swedish parliament), [New Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democracy_\(Sweden\)) was founded in 1994 by businessman [Bert Karlsson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bert_Karlsson) and aristocrat [Ian Wachtmeister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Wachtmeister). Although New Democracy promoted economic issues as its foremost concern, it also advocated restrictions on immigration and [welfare chauvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_chauvinism). The party saw a sharp rise in support in 1994 before declining soon after.[^cite_note-r3334-306][^cite_note-r54-307]

In 2010, the [Sweden Democrats](65873.md) entered parliament for the first time. The Sweden Democrats originally had connections to [white nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) during its early days but later began expelling hardline members and moderated its platform to transform itself into a more mainstream movement. The party calls for more robust immigration and asylum policies, compulsory measures to assimilate immigrants into Swedish society, and stricter law and order policies. The [Sweden Democrats](65873.md) are currently the second largest party in Sweden, with 20.5% of the popular vote in the [2022 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Swedish_general_election), and the second most seats in the [Swedish parliament](158651.md) with 72 seats.[^cite_note-Rydgren-226][^cite_note-telegraphboots-308]

#### Switzerland

See also: [Far-right politics in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_politics_in_Switzerland)

In Switzerland, the right-wing populist [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party) (SVP) reached an all-time high in the [2015 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Swiss_federal_election). The party is mainly considered [national conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESkenderovic2009p._124:_"..._and_prefers_to_use_terms_such_as_''national-conservative''_or_''conservative-right''_in_defining_the_SVP._In_particular,_''national-conservative''_has_gained_prominence_among_the_definitions_used_in_Swiss_research_on_the_SVP"-309][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeden200695-310] but it has also variously been identified as "extreme right"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIgnazi2006234-311] and "radical right-wing populist",[^cite_note-312] reflecting a spectrum of ideologies among its members. Its far-right wing includes members such as [Ulrich Schlüer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulrich_Schl%C3%BCer) and [Pascal Junod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pascal_Junod?action=edit\&redlink=1), who heads a [New Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neue_Rechte) study group and has been linked to [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) and neo-Nazism.[^cite_note-tau.ac.il-313][^cite_note-314]

In Switzerland, radical right populist parties held close to 10% of the popular vote in 1971, were reduced to below 2% by 1979, and grew to more than 10% in 1991. Since 1991, these parties (the [Swiss Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Democrats) and the [Swiss Freedom Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Freedom_Party)) have been absorbed by the SVP. During the 1990s, the SVP grew from the fourth largest party to the largest and gained a second seat in the [Swiss Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Council) in 2003 with the prominent politician and businessman [Christoph Blocher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christoph_Blocher). In 2015, the SVP received 29.4% of the vote, the highest vote ever recorded for a single party throughout Swiss parliamentary history.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

#### United Kingdom

See also: [Radical right (Europe) § United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\)#United_Kingdom)



[Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage), leader of [Reform UK](59757202.md)

The scholarly authors Breeze, Bale, Ashkenas and Aisch, and Clarke et al. characterised the [UK Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Independence_Party) (UKIP), then led by [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage), as a right-wing populist party.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322] UKIP campaigned for an [exit](41688778.md) from the European Union prior to the [2016 European membership referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_Kingdom_European_Union_membership_referendum)[^cite_note-323] and a [points-based immigration system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Points-based_immigration_system_\(United_Kingdom\)) similar to that used in Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoodwinMilazzo2015172,_231Heywood2015139-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-mirror-326] In the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), UKIP entered candidates in 44 of the 650 available seats, winning none of them, and achieving 0.1% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-327] In 2013, the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), which along with the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)) governed from 2010 to 2015 as a [coalition government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition), saw local party campaigners pledging support for UKIP over issues related to the [European Union](33053109.md) and gay marriage.[^cite_note-328]

The role of UKIP in the UK underwent a rapid transformation post-[Brexit](41688778.md), with Nigel Farage leading the initiative to establish the Brexit Party, which was subsequently rebranded as [Reform UK](59757202.md). These entities have consistently been identified as extensions of UKIP,[^cite_note-329] sharing common populist ideological elements.[^cite_note-330] After winning 14% of the vote in the [2024 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election), political scientist [Tim Bale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Bale) described Reform UK party leader Farage as the "British representative of the populist [radical right in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(Europe\))", as one of the moderate far-right parties in Europe.[^cite_note-331] [Cas Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde), an expert on populism and extremism, categorised the party as radical right due to the opposition towards "fundamental elements of [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy)".[^cite_note-332] In *[British Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Politics_\(journal\))*, political scientist Peter Dorey attributed the electoral decline of the Conservative Party as being intensified by the rise of Reform UK, as a party on the "authoritarian populist Right".[^cite_note-333] The party's most radical faction has since splintered into [Restore Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restore_Britain), With its leader [Rupert Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert_Lowe) becoming a rising star in british politics.

In the Conservative Party, [Thatcherism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatcherism) had right-wing populist elements, including [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_nationalism) and [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_conservatism)[^cite_note-334] Although [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher) has been characterised by some scholars as a right-wing populist politician in the UK,[^cite_note-Gifford_2006_851–869-335][^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337] this has been disputed by other scholars due to its applicability in the context of the 1980s.[^cite_note-338] Others contend that Thatcher's role was pivotal in steering the party's ideology towards a more populist direction.[^cite_note-339] The phenomenon is commonly referred to as "Thatcherite populism".[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-Gifford_2006_851–869-335][^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] Other prominent right-wing populists in the party include past prime minister [Boris Johnson](19065069.md)[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345] and [Jacob Rees-Mogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Rees-Mogg), the former [Secretary of State for Business, Energy and Industrial Strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Business,_Energy_and_Industrial_Strategy).[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]

Ingle and Swanson, et al. consider the Northern Ireland [Democratic Unionist Party](221428.md) (DUP) to be a right-wing populist party.[^cite_note-dup-350][^cite_note-Swanson-351]

[^cite_note-37]: &#x20;[Mudde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cas_Mudde): authoritarianism "is the belief in a strictly ordered society, in which infringements of authority are to be punished severely. In this interpretation, \[it] includes law and order and "punitive conventional moralism". It does not necessarily mean an anti-democratic attitude, but neither does it preclude one. In addition, the authoritarian's submission to authority, established or not, is "not absolute, automatic, nor blind". In other words, while authoritarians will be more inclined to accept (established) authority than non-authoritarians, they can and will rebel under certain circumstances."

[^cite_note-38]: &#x20;"Maximal" right-wing populists here give a preference for the *[état légal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tat_l%C3%A9gal)*—which gives primacy to the law as expressed by the general will via election or referendum; against the *[Rechtsstaat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rechtsstaat)*—which limits the power of the democratic state (the majority) to protect the rights of minorities.

A leftist leader can also be both authoritarian and populist, but the term *authoritarian populism*, which Stuart Hall coined, can be applied as a distinctive version of far-right populism.

[^cite_note-148]: &#x20;["Opinion: There are several good reasons Pierre Poilievre will never be prime minister — and one reason he just might"](https://www.thestar.com/opinion/contributors/there-are-several-good-reasons-pierre-poilievre-will-never-be-prime-minister-and-one-reason/article_1f27e9a3-f45a-51f6-b872-f05e20f8be7c.html). *Toronto Star*. 19 June 2022.

[^cite_note-149]: &#x20;["NP View: The unstoppable Pierre Poilievre"](https://nationalpost.com/opinion/np-view-the-unstoppable-pierre-poilievre). *[National Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Post)*. 5 August 2022. Trying to demonize Poilievre as a "populist" or as Canada's Trump, or implying that he is a white supremacist or opposed to women's rights is unlikely to succeed. He is pro-choice, pro-immigration and has forcefully denounced white replacement theory and all of "that kind of thinking."

[^cite_note-150]: &#x20;Forrest, Maura (12 September 2022). ["The quick take on Canada's new Conservative leader"](https://www.politico.com/news/2022/09/12/canada-conservative-leader-poilievre-00056205). *Politico*. He has been compared to former President Donald Trump for his populist overtures, but in terms of substance, he has largely confined himself to pocketbook issues. He is pro-immigration — his wife is a Venezuelan immigrant — and now calls himself pro-choice.

[^cite_note-151]: &#x20;["Is there room for centrists in the current Conservative Party?: Tasha Kheiriddin on the right path forward for Conservatives in Canada"](https://thehub.ca/2022-08-09/is-there-any-room-for-centrists-in-the-current-conservative-party-tasha-kheiriddin-on-the-right-path-forward-for-conservatives-in-canada/). *The Hub*. 9 August 2022. And second, what parts of his program—which, to be fair to him, he is pro-immigration, pro-same-sex marriage, and pro-choice—do you take exception to?

[^cite_note-152]: &#x20;McConkey, David (23 October 2022). ["Pierre Poilievre, populist politician?"](https://www.brandonsun.com/opinion/2022/10/24/pierre-poilievre-populist-politician). *[The Brandon Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Brandon_Sun)*. In several ways, Poilievre does not fit the mould of a new populist. For one, Poilievre is not new. He was a cabinet minister in the Stephen Harper government and he has been a member of Parliament for almost 20 years. For another, he is not your stereotypical reactionary. He is at ease with the non-traditional family, he is pro-choice, he is pro-immigration.

[^cite_note-153]: &#x20;Campbell, Clark (16 September 2022). ["The making of Pierre Poilievre, conservative proselytizer"](https://www.theglobeandmail.com/politics/article-the-making-of-pierre-poilievre-conservative-proselytizer/). *[The Globe and Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Globe_and_Mail)*. But he is no Donald Trump in tenets or temperament. He doesn't echo the anti-immigrant rhetoric, and abhors Mr. Trump's gargantuan deficits. He is so calculated that he could never be the erratic bundle of impulses that rambles at a Trump rally.

[^cite_note-154]: &#x20;["Canada's Conservatives pick a brainy brawler as leader"](https://www.economist.com/the-americas/2022/09/15/canadas-conservatives-pick-a-brainy-brawler-as-leader). *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*. 15 September 2022. His rhetorical style evokes populists such as Donald Trump. But his enemies list is more circumscribed. Unlike Mr Trump, he favours immigration.

[^cite_note-155]: &#x20;Moore, Samuel (4 November 2022). ["Pierre Poilievre: Canada's next Prime Minister?"](https://cherwell.org/2022/11/04/pierre-poilievre-canadas-next-pm/). *[Cherwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherwell_\(newspaper\))*. Moreover, in a way that distinguishes him from Trump and other right-wing populists, Poilievre's social policies are progressive. He is pro-choice and pro-LGBT rights and has actually criticised the Trudeau ministry for not being pro-immigration enough, belittling the inefficiencies of the current immigration system as yet another example of big government "gatekeeping".
